Living beyond the limits of survival: wood ants trapped in a gigantic pitfall

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Where Readers Share Great Links. Join Free

A study of a wood ant population (Formica polyctena) stranded in an old underground bunker after falling through a ventilation pipe. Cut off from the outdoor nest, the ants maintained a mound in darkness and survived for years, with new workers continually replacing those that died.

The renewable energy industry weighs in on the midterms

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Democratic legislators have wrested the majority of both the House and Senate from the Republicans’ grasp following a historic midterm election this week. The clean energy industry is clamoring for a sea change in federal support for renewables after the Trump administration razed incentives and funding since taking office in 2025.

While each candidate’s stance on clean energy isn’t apparent — nor is the effect this midterm election will have on domestic renewable energy markets — Democrats and left-leaning legislators have historically supported renewables more than their counterparts across the political aisle.

Wood Mackenzie is predicting that permitting reform will be a high priority for this new class of federal legislators, because of the rising costs of fuel and electricity from oil supply pressures brought on by the United States’ war with Iran and data center proliferation. However, the firm is also predicting that President Donald Trump could exercise his executive power to hamper proposed federal renewable energy subsidies.

“While we see the likelihood for permitting reform, how policy is legislated and implemented remains a risk to the U.S. economy,” said David Brown, director, global integrated research for Wood Mackenzie. “Entrenched partisanship could fuel policy stagnation and regulatory confusion.”

Representatives from the renewable energy industry and environmental organizations provided statements following the midterm results. Heres’ what they had to say.

Energy affordability was a key issue in this election, reflecting the real pressures facing American households and businesses. The solar and storage industry looks forward to working with newly elected federal and state leaders to bring more affordable, reliable power online and deliver the energy savings Americans are demanding.

– Tim Pawlenty, CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association

Families and businesses need affordable, reliable power, and strengthening America’s solar manufacturing base in a bipartisan manner will help deliver lower electricity bills. Over the past several years, American manufacturers have invested billions of dollars in new factories, expanded domestic production and created good-paying jobs in communities all across the country. With electricity demand rising, solar remains one of the fastest and most affordable ways to bring new power onto the grid, and the next Congress has an opportunity to ensure that power is American-made.

That starts with reinstating the domestic content incentives, so factories Congress helped bring online have a strong domestic market for American-made solar. It also means robust enforcement of our trade laws to ensure a level playing field for American solar manufacturers and continued investment in next-generation technologies so we can compete with, and leapfrog, China. We look forward to working with the new Congress to advance these shared priorities, keep electricity affordable for American families and strengthen our energy security and industrial workforce.

– Yogin Kothari, chief Strategy officer of the SEMA Coalition

Today, the clean power industry directly employs more than 400,000 Americans, and the U.S. has nearly 388 GW of clean power online, enough to power more than 83 million homes — but we’ll need a lot more than that to meet skyrocketing demand. Modernizing our outdated permitting process is key to unlocking over $800 billion in new infrastructure and 350-plus GW of new clean energy, keeping prices in check and the lights on at home for families and businesses across the country.

– Jason Grumet, CEO of the American Clean Power Association

Since their inception, competitive power markets have delivered billions in consumer savings while spurring innovation. They are the best way to make sure power generation investments are disciplined and efficient, with companies, not ratepayers, bearing the risk. EPSA looks forward to working with lawmakers on both sides of the aisle on policies that keep the grid reliable, protect customers and bring down costs in the long run.

– Todd Snitchler, president and CEO of the Electric Power Supply Association

The message from voters is clear: Americans want greater economic security, relief from rising costs and an energy system that works for them. This election put a bright spotlight on how this country’s outdated energy system and dependence on fossil fuels leave families and businesses exposed to unaffordable price shocks … Some of the biggest opportunities for progress may come at the state and city level. New governors, state legislatures and mayors have the power to move quickly on solutions that address the needs of their constituents: strengthening local economies, creating jobs and lowering bills. From ensuring data centers provide real benefits to communities to expanding clean energy, modernizing the grid, enabling affordable and accessible housing, and improving public transit, state and local leaders can walk-the-talk that climate action delivers real benefits for people.

– David Widawsky, director of World Resources Institute United States

Regardless of election outcomes, access to affordable, reliable energy and energy infrastructure remains essential to nearly every aspect of our daily lives, from powering our homes and businesses to keeping the cost of everyday goods and services within reach. As these leaders prepare to take office, we encourage them to make energy access, affordability, and reliability a priority.

We are ready to work with policymakers on both sides of the aisle to advance policies that support the development of critical energy infrastructure to create jobs, strengthen our nation’s energy security and ensure families and businesses have access to the energy they need.

– David Holt, president of the Consumer Energy Alliance

We just witnessed the collective power voters showed to beat back the Trump regime and fight for their country. Trump and his GOP cronies tried to drive our economy into the ground, but the American people stood up and made it clear that they reject Trump’s disastrous agenda.

We are committed to working with the incoming Congress to hold the administration accountable for the harm they have done to working people and pass legislation to build a clean economy that works for us all — not just billionaires. Creating opportunity for workers and communities while addressing environmental challenges is not just good policy, it’s also good politics.

– Jason Walsh, executive director of the BlueGreen Alliance

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

'We’ve been a shadow' - Charlton Athletic boss Nathan Jones' admission ahead of Sheffield United test

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Nathan Jones has conceded his Charlton Athletic side have been “a shadow” ahead of their clash with Sheffield United tomorrow (November 7).

Chris Wilder’s Blades appear to have turned a corner in the Championship, while Jones’ Addicks are reeling after four straight league defeats.

Did you know with a Digital subscription to Yorkshire Post, you can get access to all of our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

They were beaten by Bolton Wanderers earlier this week having previously been seen off by Middlesbrough, Norwich City and Watford.

“It’s always about us,” said Jones. “Yes, we have to nullify opposition threats but it’s always about us and that’s the thing that we can affect.

“That’s what we haven’t been [doing]. We’ve been a shadow of us over these last three or four games.

“I get paid to do this. This is what I get paid to do Part of that, as the manager, is leading a group and getting the best out of a group. I get applauded when we do well and overachieve, and the buck falls with me when we’re not. So I’m really looking forward to the challenge.

“If my players are as ready as I am, then we’ll be difficult to beat. Some of the performances have been there, but we’ve not been us on a regular basis. If we are us, we can compete.”

Sheffield United will be looking to capitalise upon Charlton’s error-prone nature, as Jones’ men have developed a nasty habit of gifting goals away.

“First and foremost, you’ve got to put in a full performance,” said Jones. “A full performance means that you don’t make big errors and you don’t gift goals.

“You make people work, and then you can rely on your structure and the work that you do to shut out the opposition.

“When you do the work in possession and all the attacking work, then you just want that to be executed on the day and help us to score goals. We’ve not been doing that enough. When we’ve scored, we’ve not defended. When we’ve made mistakes, we have been punished. But that’s our fault for making mistakes.”

Small retailers brace for 'huge hit' this holiday season as shipping costs spike

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

When Jenni Shields began her small business eight years ago selling handmade hair accessories, she paid $2.93 to ship an order, or close to $4 in today's dollars when adjusted for inflation.

Last year, Shields said, an average shipping label for her products cost around $4.50. But when she took a typical batch of orders to her local U.S. Postal Service office last month in Kansas City, Kansas, she was shocked to see that it was an average of nearly $6 per package.

Shields is just one of the small- and medium-sized retailers who are struggling to keep up with rising shipping costs as the price of fuel continues to trend upward.

Small business owners such as Shields are facing a difficult holiday season. Customer demand is strong, but some retailers said their profits will be dented — if not eaten up entirely — by higher shipping and other costs.

Shields said she charges her customers $6 in shipping fees, which includes roughly $1.50 to cover her packaging supplies. The dramatic upswing this year means she's no longer breaking even on her orders and instead eating the added costs, she said.

"I am losing money at this point, and I've really taken a huge hit this year," said Shields, the owner of Evie Pearl Handmade, which sells bows, quilted headbands and other accessories. "I feel like it's just been this slow increase up [in shipping costs], and I feel horrible."

The surge in fuel prices since the U.S. and Israel attacked Iran in February has driven up the cost of shipping by sea, air, rail and road.

That means higher costs across the board, including for shipping giant Amazon, which reported an 11% increase in shipping expenses in the third quarter compared with last year, to individual retailers.

Globally, air cargo is going for about $3.37 per kilogram, up 43% from a similar period a year ago, according to transportation marketplace Freightos. Judah Levine, Freightos' head of research, said in addition to higher fuel, demand for fast shipping of hardware used to build data centers is helping to boost air freight rates, even weeks before peak last-minute holiday packages take to the skies.

For businesses, that runup is going to "squeeze their margins in the near-term," said Jason Seidl, managing director and transportation analyst at TD Cowen. "Larger companies tend not to be exposed to the spot market, and they'll have what we call dedicated deals in place."

But that doesn't help smaller companies, which are walking a delicate line of not wanting to alienate customers who have enjoyed free shipping for nearly a generation.

Todd Shelton, founder of the eponymous 23-year-old New Jersey clothing company that makes high-end men's jeans, T-shirts and button-up shirts locally, said he started charging customers for return shipping this week for the first time after absorbing the higher expenses this year. The shipping is deducted from customers' refunds.

Shelton said his costs have been rising for materials, too, such as his imported Japanese denim and shirt fabric from Portugal. The company is still profitable, but the higher costs have eaten into profit margins as they stay elevated, he's considering raising prices or charging for shipping to customers, he said.

Shields said she's considering raising her shipping fees for the first time in six years to keep up with the increases.

The holiday season is one of Shields' busiest. But she said she's seen a slowdown in sales because of shipping fees.

"I can't compete with Amazon on the free shipping. I can't do that. I can't afford that," she said. "I'm seeing so much of my percentage that's supposed to come back to me and my family being put towards the shipping costs."

With no sign of fuel prices declining significantly, Shields, who runs her business out of her spare bedroom at home, said she's not sure that small businesses will be able to emerge on the other side of the holidays.

Shippers including UPS, FedEx and USPS have all instituted holiday surcharges, with prices varying by package type and lasting through the end of the season. Though that's typical during the peak holiday period each year, those charges have continued to rise over the past few years.

But even as costs everywhere climb, consumers are still spending.

According to the National Retail Federation, retail sales through the end of the year are expected to grow 4.2%, translating to total spending of $1.01 trillion. That's compared with last year's holiday sales totaling $972 billion, which was 3.1% growth.

Some of that growth is from inflation, meaning consumers are simply paying more for the same goods.

"Customers all expect to spend more this holiday, but part of why they expect to spend more is they expect things to cost more, not because they'd like to spend more or they were hoping to get more quantity of stuff," said Aaron Cheris, the global retail practice head at Bain & Company. "So what I suspect happens is that non-discretionary categories do fine, because you buy the food you were going to need to buy anyway, but then the amount of cash that's left over for [discretionary] goods is less."

Still, Cheris said it's shaping up to be a record year for shipping costs, which is putting immense pressure on smaller retailers. That means many retailers may find themselves in financial distress in the first quarter of next year as they struggle to make it through the holiday season.

He added that larger retail companies will also have to reckon with rising costs, though they may not feel those effects until later quarters because they have shipping contracts that have already been locked in and have control over their own delivery fleets.

"If anything, it benefits the scale players over the little players, but it's kind of a little bit bad for everybody," Cheris said. "I think that is the challenge."

e.l.f.’s stop-motion Christmas ad is a win for human creativity

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Apartment D Films’ Cami Kwan discusses the intricate process behind the handcrafted film.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The fight for the Christmas advert crown is on, and brands have set standards high this year. From hand-drawn animation to immersive storytelling, there's been a wonderful display of hand-crafted creativity, and among the ranks, beauty brand e.l.f. did not disappoint.

As the brand's first-ever holiday campaign, the beautiful stop-motion animation is a sparkling debut that captures the joy of the festive season. A masterful display of tactile craftsmanship, the 'e.l.f. Made to Stand Out' campaign carries many of the traits of the best Christmas adverts, proving that analogue storytelling is the perfect tool for sparking festive joy.

Narrated by iconic actor Alan Cumming, the campaign follows friends Kit, Echo and Cleo as they reunite in their hometown for a holiday get-together. What could be an uncomfortable hangout turns into a celebration of the qualities that make them extraordinary, spreading festive cheer.

“For a campaign about the power of standing out, the medium needed to stand out too, and stop motion felt like such a natural fit for this story," explains Oshiya Savur, chief marketing officer at e.l.f. Beauty. "We're seeing a real appetite for nostalgia and a return to simpler times, with 72% of Americans saying nostalgic memories inspire them when life feels complicated.

There's an incredible level of craftsmanship and character that comes from building an entire world by hand

There's something about stop motion that takes you right back to the holiday specials you grew up watching, but we saw an opportunity to give that familiar art form a uniquely e.l.f. twist. There's an incredible level of craftsmanship and character that comes from building an entire world by hand, and when you combine that with our signature wit and wink, you create something that feels both familiar and completely unexpected.”

Created by Apartment D Films, each element of the ad reads as a love letter to analogue craft, from individually moulded characters to intricately constructed miniature sets. "We used a wide variety of different materials! It's truly a mixed media project," Cami Kwan, Creative Director of Apartment D, tells Creative Bloq. "The puppets have aluminium and steel wire armatures inside, which makes them posable. Their hands and heads are made of printed resin, which we then painted and finished with actual e.l.f. products! The cheeks and mouths of the puppets are colored using a combination of e.l.f. Powder Blush Palette, e.l.f Perfect 10 Eyeshadow Palette, and e.l.f. Halo Glow Silky Powder Highlighter.

All the characters' hair is made with different weights and textures of yarn, to call back to super nostalgic toys, but with a modern sensibility. The eyebrows and eyelids of the characters are made of a mix of wax and clay, so they're super mouldable to get big expressions in the performance, and also so you can literally see the animators' fingerprints on the characters as they emote.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

A lot of the world around the character is made of wood, but with hand-painted textures so the wood grain is more painterly and stylized. The mini products and bottles the characters use are all hand-sculpted out of clay, and then finished with a variety of nail art techniques, like cured acrylic, gels, and powder shimmers! The mini bottles are really so, so small; none of them is more than 1 inch tall."

It wasn't a project without some difficulties, as Cami explains. "One of our challenges was integrating real beauty products intended to live on human skin with stop-motion puppets. Unlike real humans, our puppets don't have pores and don't have a body temperature, both of which e.l.f. beauty products are chemically formulated to interact with.

On top of that, stop motion puppets are designed to be handled and touched constantly during the animation process, so the treatments and colors on them need to be incredibly resilient to constant wear and tear. We spent a good deal of time on the material testing of the e.l.f. beauty products to see which ones would integrate best with our fabrication techniques.

We're seeing a renewed appreciation for the analog, and I think that's a natural response to an increasingly digital world

It was actually really interesting to let our puppet team dip into their extensive knowledge of material science! We've found that generally anything based in a powder pigment merged really well with our typical techniques, and those combinations were really successful in getting puppets with a lot of character and a lot of dynamic color and shimmer on screen!"

Honouring such a tactile animation style is a time intensive practice, but are audiences hungry for analogue creativity? “Absolutely," says Oshiya. "The more purely AI-generated content we see, the greater the opposite and equal reaction from people craving something real: human connection and creativity they can actually feel.

We're seeing a renewed appreciation for the analogue, and I think that's a natural response to an increasingly digital world. At e.l.f., we're human first, human last. AI can be an incredible tool, but taste, discernment and original ideas come from human ingenuity. At the end of the day, we want to make people feel something, and that human element is what makes it possible.”

"The thing I'm most proud of is how kind and collaborative our crew on this project was. It's not something you can point to on screen, but it's something you can feel in how much care was taken with every frame, and how lively the animation is. There was so much joy in the creation and filming of this project, and I do think you can feel that in how kinetic and inventive everything is. It's a little zany, a little imperfect, and really so captivating when all seen together!" says Cami.

Every quirk and detail adds personality to the story, and there's no expectation of perfection.

"For a more specific on-screen detail, though, I love the smear frames we used when Echo is zooming between the tables and getting everyone amped up! Smear frames are an animation technique that's most typical in 2D animation – they're moments when, for just a few frames, you distort the character and literally smear them to give a sense of speed, mimicking the way something moving too fast on a live-action camera will appear blurry.

You hardly ever see smear frames in stop motion, but one of the things that we really focus on at Apartment D is pushing the stylistic boundaries of stop motion and finding ways to bring 2D animation techniques into our three-dimensional medium. So, in that Echo zooming scene, we actually used pieces of cut paper to create practical, dimensional smear frames! Jen Prokopowicz, the animator of that shot, curved the paper smears to get an extra sense of dimension, and created smears of different sizes to get a sense of speeding up and slowing down. It's just so cool, and something that generally only real animation nerds will notice," Kami adds.

“At e.l.f., we've never been interested in being part of a sea of sameness. We're a brand that celebrates the unexpected, embraces individuality and believes that what makes us different is what makes us extraordinary. That's what I love about stop motion. Every quirk and detail adds personality to the story, and there's no expectation of perfection. Just as no two handcrafted creations are exactly alike, no two people are either. It's a playful way to bring our belief in celebrating every eye, lip and face to life, and a reminder that your difference is low-key your gift,” says Oshiya.

For more creative inspiration check out Prada's Disney inspired Christmas campaign or take a look at Dior's festive ad that ruffled some feathers.

Tracing Wild Horse Nine's Route Through Chile—From Santiago to Rapa Nui

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Within a few minutes of arriving at archaeological site Ahu Tongariki in Rapa Nui, where 15 gargantuan moai statues stand with their backs to the sea, my guide Gina’s phone lights up with the words “Tsunami Alert.” I ask her if this is a relatively normal occurrence on the island. “No… Although there was one [tsunami] in 1960 that washed several of these away towards the hill,” she says, gesturing towards the large sacred monolithic heads carved from volcanic stone, the Pacific Ocean sitting vast and blue behind them. The alert was later lifted, but I still felt a sense of impending doom as I posed for a photo in front of the moai.

Martin McDonagh's new film, starring Sam Rockwell and John Malkovich, follows two CIA agents through Chile.

Flirting with one’s own mortality at the edge of the world sits right at the heart of Wild Horse Nine, a new film written and directed by Martin McDonagh and filmed partially on Rapa Nui. Starring Sam Rockwell and John Malkovich, the dark comedy charts the journey of two CIA agents in Chile as they travel from their station in Santiago to the Polynesian island for a week-long vacation. Their trip also happens to be just before the country’s 1973 military coup, which would oust socialist president Salvador Allende and install dictator General Augusto Pinochet—an event that, for those at all familiar with the history of American foreign policy in Latin America, these two agents have clearly meddled in as operatives. I’d come to Chile to follow in the film's wake—to trace the real-life locations and history behind the silver-screen fiction.

The film’s characters Chris Carlisle (Malkovich) and Lee Chesterfield (Rockwell) form the kind of acerbic buddy duo that’s become associated with McDonagh films like In Bruges. The older, brasher Chris, now in the waning years of his spy career, considers their trip to Rapa Nui his final act following a terminal cancer diagnosis, bullishly demanding to see “the big heads” in reference to the moai. Lee, meanwhile, functions as exasperated colleague, grieving friend, and increasingly, minder, as his mentor’s memory begins to slip, sometimes returning loudly in moments of unguarded truth—or perhaps deathbed confessions. (One such example: a monologue where he cheerily reminisces about other elected governments he’d helped overthrow on the job.)

Chris and Lee are an uncurious pair of travelers, bristling at polite requests to educate themselves about Rapa Nui's Indigenous culture, and showing little interest in taking its history seriously. (“Why are you here if you’re just going to make fun of the place?” a fellow tourist asks Chris.) They play the part of ugly American tourists astutely—and yet, it’s largely through their eyes that we see both a 1970's Santiago and Rapa Nui on the screen.

Chris Carlisle (Malkovich) and Lee Chesterfield (Rockwell) travel from Santiago to Rapa Nui once character Chris receives a teminal diagnosis.

The rising interest in traveling to film locations is why tour operator Abercrombie & Kent has launched a 9-day itinerary, which allows guests to chase Wild Horse Nine's trail. I traveled to Chile to road-test the new offering. And like the characters of Carlisle and Chesterfield, I begin my trip in Santiago.

Unlike in the movie, the city is experiencing an unseasonable amount of rain on the morning I arrive. The Andes, which usually loom imposingly over the skyline, are concealed behind thick clouds, and the Mapocho River, which cuts through the city like an artery, is close to bursting its banks. But the 19th-century architecture of bohemian Barrio Lastarria is much more familiar. I’m met outside my hotel by my guide Lisette Matamala, who will spend the next couple of days introducing me to the fraught political history that serves as the backbone of Wild Horse Nine’s suspenseful plot.

The rain is lashing down, but we forge ahead on foot to nearby Plaza de la Constitución, and stand under comically sized umbrellas to have a look at La Moneda Palace. The seat of the Chilean president, this palace was bombed during the coup, resulting in one of the most memorable images from that day. In the movie, viewers see the regal structure, still intact, glimpsed through the office window of cranky CIA bureau chief MJ (Steve Buscemi), a key player in orchestrating the chaos. According to production designer Denis Schnegg, who I will speak to later on the phone, the team filmed that scene in an office opposite the palace that really had been used by the CIA during that time: “To stand in that room and look out at the palace, knowing how these people had been orchestrating it from their window, that the whole thing was planned—it's quite chilling.”

Santiago's presidential palace La Moneda in the aftermath of the 1973 coup d'etat led by Commander of the Army General Augusto Pinochet.

The consequences of their work are etched within the Salvador Allende Museum of Solidarity, a contemporary art museum in the historic neighborhood of Barrio República that has lived several lives: First, as an affluent mid-20th-century family home; then a left wing exhibition space founded in 1971 by a group of creative intellectuals; and, later, as a detention center and listening station operated by Pinochet’s Directorate of National Intelligence (DINA). It has since returned to its original form as a museum, resurrected after the return to democracy in 1990.

While not a Wild Horse Nine filming location, it’s an important stop to help place the film within the context of these very real historical events. “This house has a lot of layers of history and meaning,” museum director María José Lemaitre tells me as we walk down a flight of wide, winding wooden stairs. “And also, darkness.” When the art was reinstalled, secrets were uncovered within the bones of the building: transcriptions of tapped phone calls were hidden by DINA in the ceilings, and phone-tapping equipment sat in the basement.

Lemaitre and her colleague, museum curator Caroll Yasky, bring me to see the latter, housed in a dusty, windowless subterranean room. We linger over the handwritten names and phone numbers of families the DINA had spied on—tangible evidence of the horrors enacted by the history driving McDonagh’s plot. “The museum may show the past, but what is important is the future,” says Yasky as we walk back upstairs into the daylight.

The fingerprints of Pinochet’s regime are never far away in Santiago. I spend a pisco sour-filled evening dining with a university professor whose father had been one of the dictator's driver—a role that, he suggests, haunted his father so deeply he had a stroke to forget it. (It's an anecdote I can’t help but thread back to Chris’ struggles with redemption in Wild Horse Nine, which we often see him exploring in misguided attempts to write a memoir that he plans to leave behind following his imminent death.)

Much of Rapa Nui feels—and appears in the film—as though it sits on the edge of the world.

Another afternoon, we visit Villa Grimaldi, a once-notorious detention center on the outskirts of the city that now serves as a memorial. Two of its survivors are Alvaro Ahumada and Ignacio Puelma, smartly dressed and in their early 70s, who meet us under the trees near the entrance for a tour. We pass sobering remnants of the detention center’s structures, but also clusters of fragrant roses, colorful mosaics, and water features. “We didn’t want to have a horror museum,” says Ahumada, who is also the president of the foundation. “Instead, we wanted to wash away what happened here.”

The pair, I realize, would have been around the same age as the character of a young, optimistic left-wing student in the film named Monica, played by Mariana di Girolamo. These two were both members of the student-led Movement of the Revolutionary Left, and at just 19 and 21 at the time, they had been transferred from Villa Grimaldi to the same concentration camp where they connected over music, even forming a band during their imprisonment. “Our bond is very deep,” says Puelma, as a flock of squawking parakeets fly over us. “We were both rockers, so we had a lot to talk about,” adds Ahumada. “Helping each other, and looking after each other, was an act of resistance.”

Their story evokes a poignant scene in Wild Horse Nine, when they later reach Rapa Nui, during which Chris meets up with Monica at a low-lit, moody bar. As the ill-fated folk singer Victor Jara (Aron Hernandez) performs on stage, Chris tells her with disturbing frankness that he is not just a CIA agent, but one who has come down to Chile to “destabilize your democracy,” a direct reference to the United States’ involvement in installing Pinochet.

In one of the film's scenes, young Chileans are confronted by Chris's character, who tells them he has come to Chile to “destabilize your democracy.”

McDonagh’s decision to depict Jara, who was murdered by members of Pinochet's regime, is yet another reminder that beyond the buffoonery of Chris and Lee, lie the stories of real people like Ahumada and Puelma. Some of the film’s cast still feel its impact, too. “Monica [is based on] a real character: Comandante Tamara, a left-wing student and [guerilla leader] who attempted to assassinate Pinochet. And, of course, we know what happened to Victor Jara, the singer who is playing a Violeta Parra song in that bar,” di Girolamo tells me after the trip. “I was born in the first year of democracy, but my parents were students in school in ‘73. [This history] is just something you grow up with, and it's still an open wound for us.”

I arrive in Rapa Nui the same way Chris and Lee do: on vacation, via the one daily flight from Santiago, landing at a thatched airport where ukulele music wafts through the air and chickens toddle around baggage claim. Gina Pakarati, who was born and raised on the island, picks me up outside in her white Suzuki Jimny, and we set off to visit the same moai sites that, in the film, Chris tells Lee he wants to see before he dies. In fact, it’s when they first reach the island that it’s revealed to viewers that Chris has asked Lee to put a gun to his head and end things right here on Rapa Nui.

Wild horses run across the open, lush green plains of Rapa Nui island.

Pakarati, who knows the island like the back of her hand, blasts local Polynesian folk-country band Topatangi as we trace the rugged coastal drive captured in the film, past swathes of electric-green grassland and black volcanic rock. We pause for herds of the same chestnut-brown wild horses McDonagh frequently turns his camera to.

I wind my window down, and the air smells sweet yet woody. There is a surreal, heavenly quality to being here. At one point, Pakarati pulls over and plucks a small guava off a tree, so ripe that she can pull it apart with her hands, for us to eat at the side of the road.

As we drive up towards Ahu Akivi, one of several imposing moai sites on the island, small groups of tourists are milling around, some of whom I recognize. (Given the island’s size, you tend to cross paths with the same visitors fairly quickly.) Just like Chris and Lee, we’ve all come here for the same once-in-a-lifetime experience—albeit without a Super 8 camera and guns in holsters. But finally laying eyes on the moai feels like seeing a myth come to life. Pakarati, who is 43 and comes from a long lineage of Rapa Nui people, explains to me that of all the ceremonial platforms here, this one is particularly special because its seven statues are “looking towards Polynesia, the land where they came from.”

Towering moai made from volcanic stone—which Malkovich's character refers to as “the big heads”—are a living piece of ancient Polynesian history on Rapa Nui.

She recalls playing among the statues as a child (an activity now deterred by a rope) and hearing her grandfather’s stories of helping the visiting archeologist William Mulloy with their restoration in the 1960s. Thanks largely to Mulloy, that period drew more attention to the island and, after the first commercial flight arrived in 1967, ushered in the first real wave of tourism. Though it is a territory of Chile, life on Rapa Nui felt awfully far away from the coup, I'm told. Hotels began opening, more cars appeared on the roads, and in 1993, the first Hollywood film crew arrived. Despite the fact that the subsequent Kevin Costner-produced movie Rapa Nui was a widely criticized box-office flop, Pakarati says, economically everything changed after it, due to how many jobs were created—and she suggests that the spotlight of Wild Horse Nine might prompt another such wave of activity.

That initial Hollywood-driven boom on the island inspired hospitality ventures like still-operating, family-run Iorana Hotel. It’s an eccentric ocean-view property just outside of town that opened in the 1990s but feels frozen in the '70s. So much so that, in Wild Horse Nine, Chris and Lee both stay there, picking fights with unsuspecting tourists and waving their guns around in the rooms. Cars from the era were temporarily shipped over to drive past the property in certain shots, says Schnegg, but when it came to the interiors, which feature floor-to-ceiling wood paneling, tiki-style floral-patterned upholstery, and buttercup-yellow room doors, little was changed. (“The hotel is actually sort of disconnected from [town], which is probably why it's a bit stuck in time,” Schnegg notes.) One addition, however, were the protagonist’s rooms, which were specially built for the film. The owners plan to keep the set and turn it into a tourist attraction. “The last night [of filming] we were set up until 7 a.m. and then had a huge celebration in the morning,” says the owners' daughter, Carolina, as we take a look at the set. “I think this film will be good for the island.”

The family-run Iorana Hotel transports visitors—and viewers—straight to the 1970s.

We watch Chris and Lee ramble around many of the moai sites, but the one that stuns me most in its scale and beauty is the quarry at Rano Raraku, where hundreds of moai protrude at different levels from the hillside of an extinct volcano. Many lie solemnly in situ among the grass, while others now stand, having been hauled up into a standing position by archeologists in the ‘70s. Some are massive, and others unfinished. A view towards the coastline offers a spectacular vantage point of the fifteen statues of Ahu Tongariki, which all look back upon the quarry where they were created.

In Wild Horse Nine, we witness Chris and Lee unravel at this very site in a dramatic final shootout. I, in contrast, sit on a bench with Pakarati, the breeze making the grass shimmer (nowhere else have I been so astounded by the green of the grass), as she explains how these mammoth works were constructed and moved around the island by her ancestors. It may be a thrill to stand where the actors of Wild Horse Nine stood, but there’s much more gravity in knowing that I’m treading the same soil as those who carved the hulking statues before me. These days, Rapa Nui's archaeological sites can only be entered with an authorized guide—and I’m grateful for it as Pakarati shares her stories.

Rapa Nui provides a pensive setting for the film's character's, particularly Chris who knows his days are numbered.

Toward the film's end, Chris, death knocking on his door, describes isolated Rapa Nui as a lonely place. But I find it to be quite the opposite. On my final morning, we wake early and drive back over to Ahu Tongariki to watch the sun rise. The atmosphere is similar to gathering in anticipation of an eclipse. Locals and tourists alike set up camping chairs and drink coffee out of flasks as the sky changes hue: deep purple merging into soft blues, followed by shades of pink, ochre, and yellow bursting from behind the silhouettes of the moai.

Standing in the early dawn light, it’s easy to understand why such an epic natural landscape was used as a burial ground on ancient Rapa Nui—and why McDonagh thought a CIA agent like Chris, dying and trying to seek redemption from his darkened memories, may want to swagger over to such a place before his morally disreputable life comes to an end. Even with his internal struggle, does he have the right to such a bucket list? “When I read the script for the first time, I felt a little guilty about laughing [while they do] these terrible things,” di Girolamo had told me during our conversation. “Yet I’d felt empathy for these agents. I cried.”

The sun fixes itself high above the moai and we all clap. Another new day at the edge of the world.

Wild Rapa Nui is a 5.5-hour flight from Santiago, and feels a world away.

I flew direct from New York City to Santiago, before catching the 5.5-hour flight to Rapa Nui on LATAM Airlines—currently the only commercial flight to get you there. My itinerary was booked through tour operator Abercrombie & Kent, which has crafted an exclusive Wild Horse Nine trip tailored to the movie locations, while also connecting travelers with the real-life Chileans who lived under the Pinochet regime and want to share their stories.

Santiago: I spent three nights at the Singular Hotel, a boutique property in the center of artsy Barrio Lastarria. Rooms are spacious and comfortable, but the real winner here is the buzzy rooftop bar serving excellent pisco sours. It’s also walking distance from La Moneda Palace and the must-visit Chilean Museum of Pre-Columbian Art, which features several artifacts from Rapa Nui.

Rapa Nui: While there is an Explora property further inland, most hotels are clustered in and around the sole town of Hanga Roa, a laid-back, sunny community lined with seafood restaurants, surf shops, and cafes and bars wafting ukulele music. Check into Nayara Hangaroa for oceanfront rooms, a pool set upon volcanic rocks, and a concierge to assist with arranging excursions. Rumor has it that John Malkovich stayed here while filming Wild Horse Nine.

La Moneda Palace: A 15-minute walk from the Singular Hotel, Chile’s presidential palace is worth a pause as you pass through downtown. Buttressed by the Plaza de la Constitución and the Plaza de la Ciudadanía, it’s a largely civic area, but it’s hard not to be stirred by the location knowing, as Schnegg noted, that everything that took place here.

In Wild Horse Nine, the still-operating Municipal Theatre of Santiago is turned into an upper-class restaurant.

Municipal Theatre of Santiago: “For a pivotal scene with Chris and Lee, we turned the opera house into a sort of upper-class restaurant where [you’d imagine] the political elite would meet,” says Schnegg of the Municipal Theatre of Santiago, a beautiful, fully operational theater in the center of the city. Buy tickets for a performance to see its grand 19th-century interiors, or do as I did and request Abercrombie & Kent arrange a tour for a behind-the-scenes glimpse.

Museum of Memory and Human Rights: While not a location in Wild Horse Nine, this museum is an essential stop to confront Chile’s recent history and understand the lasting trauma of the Pinochet dictatorship. “It's an open wound for us because we didn't have justice,” says di Girolamo. “There are still mothers looking for their sons.” Set within a brutalist building near Quinta Normal Park, the museum displays testimonials from survivors, preserved artifacts, memorials to the missing, and government documents from the time—including several declassified CIA documents revealing its role in the 1973 coup.

Iorana Hotel: One of the main locations in Wild Horse Nine, this family-owned property overlooking the ocean is fully in operation and taking bookings. With furniture clad in tiki prints, retro wood paneling, and palm trees swaying outside the windows, it’s also a fun place just to snap a couple of photos. Ask to see the set that still remains—just watch out for the grumpy resident dog, who is best admired from afar.

Anakena Beach is a scenic tourist site on Rapa Nui, and actor Mariana di Girolamo's favorite filming location.

Anakena Beach: “My favorite location in the movie is Anakena Beach,” says di Girolamo of the scene where she and Chris strike up a brief, unlikely friendship at the Rapa Nui tourist spot, where a bar serving piña coladas sits side by side with the Ahu Nau Nau moai. “That beach is so beautiful, but I was also able to go to a very special place nearby that you can only visit with someone local: the Cave of the Virgins [or Ana o Keke] set inside a cliff.”

Ahu Tongariki and Rano Raraku: These two moai sites are within short driving distance of each other (as are most sites on Rapa Nui) and make frequent appearances in the movie. Book a local authorized guide to visit them, or arrange a guided excursion through Nayara Hangaroa. And don’t rush, either: the statues and the landscape look vastly different throughout the day as the light changes. “We were filming there for weeks on end, just living with the sky and the wind and the water and the sounds and the horses,” says Schnegg. “I’d just think, is this real?”

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

Horny Grandmas Are Having a Moment

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Not long ago, the 72-year-old adult creator DD White received a message from one of her fans requesting a custom video.

She often spends her afternoons in front of a computer fulfilling all sorts of solicitations from younger men. Some want to buy the lingerie she wears in her clips. Others ask for a recording of a “stepson video” where she pretends to catch them peeking into her room or watching her shower.

The fee varies depending on the description or length of the video, ranging anywhere from $30 to $200, and if a guy wants her to say his name, “that is going to cost more because it’s not resalable,” she says from her home office in Orlando. “They want me to come on to them, to say that I’ve noticed them staring at me. That type of thing.”

But this request was a first for White. He “wanted me to be his mommy and tell him how he came out of my body, as he’s fucking me. It was just really awkward,” she says. White, who has 11 grandchildren and eight great-grandchildren, declined the request but says that sort of thing comes with the job when you are a septuagenarian sex worker.

“I am so surprised, but younger guys really want to be with older women,” she says.

Outside the Instagram-polished demographic typically associated with creators on OnlyFans and Fansly, where youth is among your most marketable assets, there is a growing demand for a different kind of digital seductress: the GILF.

Older creators have flooded adult platforms in recent years as demand for mature content has skyrocketed. On Pornhub, searches for “GILF” increased 129 percent in 2025, and “maturing MILF” was among the platform’s leading trends, according to figures the company shared with WIRED. In the US, the market for silver foxes was even more explicit: last year the top relative search term in Oregon was “horny grandma” while Maine’s was “granny sex.”

In the evenings after she’s finished sifting through messages, White turns to camming. “My shows,” she calls them. In one stream the night following the midterm elections, she dressed up in a silk-and-lace maid outfit, imploring fans to “cum and catch the action.”

White first entered the business at 65, following a decades-long career as an accountant, working with the Score Group, an adult media company in Miami that runs dozens of porn sites, including 60plusmilfs.com. She mostly works as an “independent contractor” these days, uploading content between Streamates, LoyalFans, OnlyFans, and her personal website. In all, White pulls in around $12,000 a month with the help of her daughters, who coordinate her social media posts and help make any necessary artwork.

Aging performers like Rita Daniels, 76, and Aunt Ann, a 69-year-old footwear model whose turnoffs include “mean people, liars, [and] limp dicks,” have found big audiences as the desire for GILF content grows.

Two of Aunt Ann’s explicit videos showing penetration have over 600,000 views on Pornhub. Others creators, like 66-year-old Monika Weiss, are “finally exploring every delicious desire I kept hidden for far too long,” as she writes in her OF bio. The genre has gotten so widespread across the industry that, in August, ASN—the adult entertainment company that hosts an annual awards ceremony—added a category for GILF of the Year.

White initially got into porn because it was “something fun to do” with her husband (they were swingers, and he would often post videos of their group trysts online). But after a series of unexpected financial setbacks—the family’s manufacturing business, which her daughters took over, started to suffer as the economy worsened—she realized porn was a smart alternative to help boost her retirement savings.

“It was a combination of things going bad. I realized that the retirement wasn't going to be there like I expected,” she says. “I would need to have more than just that social security check.”

Despite its challenges, platform-dependent work has provided older adults new opportunities at a second or even third career. “Some workers were pushed into the gig economy, with unemployment or forced retirement compelling them to seek out new financial lifelines,” says Brooke Duffy, who researches the intersection of social media and labor at Cornell University. Against this backdrop, she adds, “OnlyFans offered not only a stopgap but also a potential pathway through which to wrest back control over the conditions of work” by broadening the pool of participants through a new funding model.

Older women aren’t the only creators benefiting from a resurgent interest in mature porn. When Rick Kelson was let go from his contract position at NASA in 2021, where he worked as an engineer, sex work became his main source of income, “essentially a full-time job,” he tells WIRED. It was during this period that “the market for more mature performers, daddy types, was really starting to take off,” and a friend who’d worked with various gay film studios, “thought I had exactly the look that they wanted,” Kelson says.

Broad-shouldered with a thick white beard, Kelson, who turns 60 next year, believes the industry has finally caught up to mature porn because studios realize that it isn’t the niche genre they previously wrote it off as. “The mainstream traditional studios weren’t interested in playing to that market. As far as making content available, it just didn't seem like a profitable option before. They didn’t realize how large it was.”

Kelson averages $4,000 to $5,000 a month posting between OnlyFans, JustForFans, Pornhub, and RawFuckClub, on top of personal training and masseuse work. But despite the steady stream of income he has been able to build for himself, he acknowledges how difficult it is to make a living wage in this line of work “unless you are really, really hustling and pushing some boundaries, doing some of the real hard legwork.”

Today, more than a quarter of adults over 50 rely on side hustles to survive, according to a 2023 AARP survey, with 44 percent of older workers saying they have experienced a “job disruption” since 2018.

Octogenarian actress Donna Mills joined OnlyFans this year. In October, the former soap star told ABC News that though she has yet to post x-rated content, she wasn’t completely ruling it out in the future. “Provocative, maybe. I’ve always been a bit spicy.” Mills told ABC that she won’t honor fan requests for videos of her brushing her teeth but noted that she planned to fulfill one specific request: “stomping grapes.”

“I think it’s sexier to be not just totally naked … and have a little imagination about it,” she said of her approach.

White, who has 40,000 Instagram followers, says what’s happening right now has a lot to do with new attitudes around aging and who gets to monetize their image online. “I realized that the generations are changing. They’re not stifling sex like they used to. That way of thinking is so different from when I was younger, and a lot of women have benefited from that.”

Even so, Kelson believes the volatility of the job market will continue to push older adults to subscription platforms to supplement their income. “More of them will see that they have that option,” he says. Mostly, he’s been ecstatic to see creators in his age bracket get into the business.

“I love it because it pushes back against the concept that as you get older, you have to lose pieces of your life that you enjoy,” he says. “We are not freaks just because we have a high libido at the age of 65 or 70 or whatever.”

Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.47 Million in Crypto From Investigations

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A former FBI agent pleaded guilty Thursday to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency with wallet credentials the bureau had collected in its own cases, the U.S. Attorney’s Office for the Eastern District of Virginia announced.

Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, entered pleas to wire fraud and money laundering in federal court in Alexandria. U.S. District Judge Patricia Tolliver Giles accepted the plea. He faces up to 20 years in prison and is scheduled for sentencing on March 4, 2027.

Court papers put the illicit haul at $2,474,380.66. Prosecutors say Yaroch made roughly 100 fraudulent transfers using credentials tied to 35 separate FBI investigations, drawing on wallets controlled by investigation subjects, adversarial nation-state actors and at least one wallet the FBI itself had created for an operation.

The Justice Department Office of the Inspector General had flagged the case in August after Yaroch’s arrest on a criminal complaint. That earlier account described a smaller set of transfers — about 10 moves totaling roughly $1 million — and noted that Yaroch self-reported the activity to two department employees on July 29, 2026. The plea figures are larger.

From 2017 through early 2025, Yaroch worked in the FBI Boston Division on a squad focused on an adversarial nation-state. From February 2025 through July 31, 2026, he served in the FBI’s Counterintelligence and Espionage Division at headquarters, with access to systems that stored cryptocurrency credentials gathered in investigations.

Beginning no later than December 2024, Yaroch used that access to move coins out of those wallets and into ones he controlled. After the transfers, he swapped the stolen cryptocurrency into other digital assets and ran further transactions meant to obscure its source, nature and ownership.

Between Nov. 25, 2025, and Jan. 13, 2026, he moved about $93,082 into one of his accounts, then shuffled funds among other accounts to cover debts, including credit cards, car payments and loans, the office said.

FOX 5 DC, citing the same U.S. Attorney’s Office release, noted the Portugal planning that appears in the plea papers. Yaroch asked ChatGPT how to maximize investment returns and where to live in the European Union. After the chatbot suggested buying a vineyard in Portugal, he gave Portuguese lawyers a power of attorney on June 15, 2026, to register him with the Portuguese Tax and Customs Authority, obtain a tax identification number and collect the password to his finance portal. He arranged travel to Portugal for September 2026.

The agreed statement of facts goes beyond the dollar figure. Yaroch’s conduct compromised multiple FBI investigations into criminal groups and nation-state adversaries, the office said, and it compromised FBI sources and methods used in foreign intelligence collection and criminal cases. It also “deprived FBI employees of their ability to carry out their functions to protect public safety and national security.”

First Assistant U.S. Attorney Theophani K. Stamos and Daniel S. Wierzbicki, special agent in charge of the FBI Washington Field Office’s Counterintelligence Division, announced the plea. Assistant U.S. Attorneys Kathleen Robeson and Russell Carlberg are prosecuting, with help from the National Security Cyber Section of the Justice Department’s National Security Division and the DOJ inspector general’s Mid-Atlantic region. The case is 1:26-CR-208 in the Eastern District of Virginia.

The admission lands in a week when federal crypto-crime stories keep stacking up — from a failed bitcoin kidnapping plot that produced its own guilty plea last month to FBI tallies putting U.S. crypto fraud losses above $11 billion in 2025, a figure the White House has used to frame a wider crackdown.

What sentencing will do with the gap between Yaroch’s July self-report and the $2.47 million plea figure is still open. So is how many of the 35 FBI cases can be repaired after an insider treated the bureau’s own wallet credentials as a personal ATM. March 4, 2027, is when Judge Giles gets to answer the first question.

Decades of clues helped scientists discover a massive freshwater lake miles beneath the ice in Antarctica

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Lake Vostok, a vast freshwater lake hidden beneath 2.5 miles of Antarctic ice, was mapped using radar, seismic data and satellite measurements.

In June 1996, a paper in Nature described a large, deep freshwater lake beneath the ice of central East Antarctica. The lake, now called Lake Vostok, lies under about 2.5 miles (4 kilometers) of ice.

The authors found that its area was about 50 percent larger than earlier estimates, with dimensions comparable to those of Lake Ontario.

The paper drew on clues that had been building for decades. They ranged from seismic soundings in the 1960s to airborne radar flights and, in 1993, satellite measurements.

The lake takes its name from Russia’s Vostok Station, which sits above its southern tip, according to Michael Studinger, then at Columbia University’s Lamont-Doherty Earth Observatory.

The former Soviet Union set up the station in 1957. It lies about 800 miles (1,300 kilometers) from the geographic South Pole, on roughly 2.3 miles (3,700 meters) of ice, according to NASA’s Earth Observatory.

About 30 people work at the station during the summer research season.

In 1983, the station’s air temperature fell to -129°F (-89°C).

Far below, the lake water sits under more than 360 times the atmospheric pressure at sea level.

Where the ice sheet crosses the lake, it goes afloat, Studinger writes. In some areas, lake water freezes onto the base of the ice.

Russian scientists suggested from seismic soundings in the 1960s that water might lie beneath the ice. Later, a joint U.S., British and Danish airborne radar project found flat reflections that pointed to water.

A 1974 to 1975 airborne radio-echo survey outlined a lake of about 3,900 square miles (10,000 square kilometers), the 1996 paper reports.

Robin Bell, a geophysicist at the Lamont-Doherty Earth Observatory, described those flights in a NASA Earthdata feature.

“Scientists flew around Antarctica in C-130 airplanes that sent electromagnetic waves through the ice sheet,” Bell said.

The flat layers in the radar records were the first signs of subglacial lakes, or water trapped beneath the ice. At the time, researchers took them for small, isolated ponds.

“Quite frankly they didn’t seem to be important,” Bell recalled.

In the 1990s, the European Remote Sensing satellite, ERS-1, produced the first comprehensive map of Antarctica’s ice surface using radar altimetry, the same feature says.

Scientists studying that map noticed an unusually flat area near the center of the ice sheet, about the size of Lake Ontario.

“The ice surface will look rugged. But if water is underneath, the ice surface will look very flat,” Bell explained.

In 1993, satellite altimetry independently confirmed the extent of the lake first outlined by radar, the 1996 authors wrote.

The paper’s five authors came from Russian and British institutions. They included Andrey Kapitsa of Moscow State University and Martin Siegert, then at the University of Wales.

Another author was glaciologist Gordon de Quetteville Robin of the University of Cambridge’s Scott Polar Research Institute.

The team combined new altimetry and radio-echo data with existing seismic data. They found that the lake was deep, with an average depth of 410 feet (125 meters) or more, and that its water was fresh.

The team also estimated that water stays in the lake for tens of thousands of years. They put the mean age of the water at about one million years.

That age counts from when the water was deposited as surface ice on the ancient Antarctic ice sheet.

The regional flow of the ice could be explained as steady flow along and over the lake.

Robin had suggested in the 1950s that geothermal heat from the bedrock could warm the base of the ice sheet. He could not prove it until ice-penetrating radar arrived in the 1970s.

The base of the ice stays warm because of geothermal heating from Earth’s interior, Siegert wrote in a 2005 Annual Review of Earth and Planetary Sciences paper. About 50 milliwatts of heat per square meter is enough to melt the base of an ice sheet 2.5 miles (4 kilometers) thick.

The same review describes the lake as over 155 miles (250 kilometers) long, with depths reaching about 0.6 miles (1 kilometer) in places. It is one of over 100 subglacial lakes identified beneath Antarctica.

In 2012, a Russian team drilled all the way down to the lake to look for microbes, NASA’s Earth Observatory reported. Scientists estimate the lake has been sealed under ice for up to 25 million years.

Studinger also calls Lake Vostok the closest terrestrial analog to Europa, the ice-covered moon of Jupiter.

The study was published in the journal Nature.

Moretto: Krösche ‘continues to push’ to bring Hardung with him to Milan – the situation

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Matteo Moretto has confirmed that Markus Krösche will look to try and bring Timmo Hardung with him to AC Milan.

In the past few days, there have been reports from a number of sources claiming that Gerry Cardinale is finally going to get the man that he has wanted for a few months. Krösche – who left Eintracht Frankfurt last month – has an agreement in place to come in and lead the management.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

The issue of the compensation to be paid to Eintracht seems to have been resolved, so now the main question concerns whether someone will come in to help him as a sporting director, another position vacant in the current set-up.

In a video on their YouTube channel, Fabrizio Romano and Matteo Moretto gave some updates on the plans for Milan moving forward, including the restructuring of the management. MilanNews relayed the words of the latter.

“The Krosche deal has been a rollercoaster ride. AC Milan had practically already finalised the deal before the summer window. It was also a question of timing and also of finances, as Eintracht Frankfurt were asking for much higher compensation than what Milan has paid so far, or will soon pay,” he said.

“Anyway, here we go: Marcus Krösche will be ​​Milan’s new Head of Football. Milan will pay compensation of between €3-4m. Official confirmation, as far as I know, will arrive in early January. However, it’s clear that Krosche will already begin working behind the scenes for AC Milan in the coming months, and then he will officially be the new Rossoneri director from January.

“Krösche fits into an organisational and sporting structure that Cardinale had already thought of once he had restructured Milan from scratch. There are other figures within the Milan structure, such as Bobby Gardiner, Almstadt, who from what I understand will remain, but it is clear that Krosche’s figure will be predominant and on a sporting level he will be the one with the most power.

“From what I understand, Krösche continues to push to try to bring in a sporting director who can add to the organisational chart and the name that continues to buzz in Krösche’s head is certainly that of his right-hand man Timmo Hardung, who is the current sporting director of Eintracht. However, it is still to be seen whether Timo Hardung will remain at Eintracht 100%.

“It is true that Eintracht are trying to give him as much power as possible, but I do not rule out Hardung’s exit starting from January or February. Milan remain on the lookout, other German clubs such as Nürnberg, Hannover or Hamburg are also interested in Hardung. Milan wants try inserting another figure, maybe a little further forward.”

After printing 40,000 whistles, this is the 3D printer I’d buy

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

One year later, the Bambu P2S Combo is the only 3D printer I can easily recommend.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

I’ve been wanting to try 3D printing, people tell me. Which printer should I get?

I finally have a fairly easy answer: the Bambu Lab P2S Combo. The auto-loading, fully enclosed touchscreen printer is one of the very few I can recommend. It’s the only one I’d buy for myself. It’s on sale for $649 through December 1st, but I’d vouch for it even at its normal $749 price.

I’ve spent an entire year testing the P2S alongside several competitors, printing 40,000 whistles for ICE whistleblowers along the way. After racking up 6,770 hours of print time across four different printers, I’m sure of my recommendation.

Some people who know 3D printers will argue with me:

But for the reasons I’ll explain below, the P2S Combo is my pick despite all of that. It’s durable, reliable, and simple to maintain and clean and forget about, even in ways my trusty Bambu P1P and the similar P1S lacked.

The P2S makes 3D printing easy. When I’m in the mood, I don’t even need to walk over to my printer half the time. I can fire it up from my phone or laptop, using Bambu’s app to send one of millions of ready-made models from the cloud. The automated material system (AMS) automatically loads one of the rolls of filament that I leave in its protected enclosure for months at at time without fear. It automatically calibrates, automatically prints, automatically winds the filament back up when it’s done.

I tell friends that no matter which printer you buy, you should buy a multi-material system “Combo” because manually loading and unloading filament is a chore; it means standing around a printer waiting for it to heat up and feeding plastic into a tube by hand. Automated filament systems can fix that. For me, it’s not about having lots of colors in a single print; it’s about knowing I can print something black, or white, or red, or my kid’s favorite sparkly purple without any further effort — because I loaded those colors weeks ago.

It is true that the $369 Elegoo Centauri Carbon 2 Combo also has automatic four-color filament feeding at a seemingly unbeatable price, and printed whistles and household items just as well as the P2S. I’ve spent 1,271 hours printing with the Centauri Carbon, and 939 hours with a Centauri Carbon 2, and they printed almost flawlessly right out of the box — no need to level a bed, no need to adjust belt tension, the printer calibrates itself.

But like other Bambu rivals, Elegoo doesn’t automatically load its filament from mostly airtight containers. I can’t leave rolls of filament in the printer for weeks or months when I’m not actively using them because they’ll quickly absorb moisture from the air, turn brittle, and jam up the printer’s tubes or nozzle.

Moisture is the primary reason I wouldn’t recommend an inexpensive Bambu A1 Combo to newcomers, either, or a Snapmaker U1, or any other attractive multicolor printer without sealed filament storage. The entire experience of using a 3D printer is friendlier when you don’t have to prep the printer before printing.

Even so, it’s pretty dang tempting to save $280 or more by going with the Elegoo. But over the course of the roughly 1,000 hours I tested the Carbon and Carbon 2 side by side with the Bambu P2S, the Elegoo printers broke down in ways that were more difficult to diagnose.

The original Carbon’s extruder had some feeding issues and I found a lot of black dust inside after taking it apart, seemingly from worn plastic gears. Just this week, my Carbon 2’s stopped working after its nozzle mysteriously crashed into the bed and it refused to auto-level itself (which would normally fix that issue). I had to take the bed apart and still couldn’t find anything wrong.

In the end, yanking and reseating four tiny fragile-and-yet-too-tight connectors inside the bed fixed that problem, but it’s not the kind of solution a consumer should have to do.

Anecdotally, my Bambu printers have led a comparatively charmed life: In 1,692 hours of printing with the P2S, I’ve had to swap out the nozzle twice, clean and relubricate the traversal rods and screws several times, wash the removable magnetic printing surfaces a bunch of times with water and dish soap, and clear several filament jams in the AMS. That’s about it.

P2S nozzle replacements are easier, too. When you jam today’s Elegoo printers, you’re going old-school, either by wrenching off a hot nozzle so you can clean or inexpensively replace it ($25 for eight nozzles) or by fishing out tiny wires and unscrewing two bolts to remove an entire $30 hotend (which includes the elements that heat the plastic) at once. With Bambu’s P2S, you can just yank off a gasket and open one spring clip to swap a whole new toolless hotend ($18) in less than a minute. It does help to have a pair of mini pliers to grab the nozzle, though.

The Elegoo printers also have noisy fans that stay active even when you’re not printing, unlike the Bambu, though the Centauri Carbon 2 does shut them off more quickly than the original. Both Elegoo and Bambu let you remotely monitor your prints from its built-in camera and capture timelapse videos, but Elegoo’s camera and lighting are crap quality by comparison.

If I’m being completely honest, Bambu’s own Bambu P1S Combo, at $509 on sale, is the closest competition for the $649 P2S Combo, and I had a hunch it might be my pick in the end. I upgraded my P1P with most of the P1S upgrades, and it’s been a reliable workhorse: nearly 3,000 hours of 3D printing so far.

But over the past year, I’ve gravitated towards the P2S more and more because of its easy-swap hotends, the built-in touchscreen where I easily manage prints and rolls of filament, and all kinds of little improvements that I find I actually care about:

Plus, I did have to replace the entire heatbed on my P1P once. I can’t say that nothing will go wrong with the P2S, and you shouldn’t take away that they’re as simple as an inkjet. They will need occasional maintenance, you’ll need to load good-quality filament and probably want to avoid rolls that come on lightweight cardboard (I try to buy Bambu, Sunlu, or Elegoo refills for my reusable plastic Bambu spools), and even then there will be components that wear out.

After 1,600 hours, the AMS 2 that came with my Bambu P2S review unit sometimes complains when trying to unwind or rewind some rolls of filament from some slots, and I suspect it’s because some of the gears are wearing prematurely. Bambu recently announced it’ll replace some of those gears free of charge, but only ones manufactured between May and July 2026, and even then you’ll have to be pretty handy to install them yourself.

I strongly believe people buying their first printer should get something tried and true, fully baked, simple to use, and the P2S is the best yet. But even Bambu is still catering more to DIY enthusiasts who’ll tolerate the occasional hardware defect.

So yes, if you’re asking “Which printer should I get,” I think you’d be pleasantly surprised with a P2S! But I wouldn’t blame you for waiting a few more years.

How we made 442GOALS

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

How did FourFourTwo make the viral game that took the long international break by storm? We reveal all

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The new three-week international break risked being a desert in football journalism – a 21-day void with little club action to talk about, and only the Nations League to occupy ourselves.

What it was though, was the perfect time for nostalgia and nonsense. That's why we chose that period to launch 442GOALS, an online draft game with a very simple concept. What if FourFourTwo… asked you to pick a 4-4-2… of players who have scored a total of 442 Premier League goals?

It felt like a sensible total: that's about 40 each, and naturally a few Harry Kanes and Mo Salahs will pick up the slack for your David Seamans and Gary Nevilles.

After all, the best games combine the serious with the silly – and this was an evening project that started with the ambition of building a dream team in which Edinson Cavani pales in comparison to Mark Noble; where sometimes, Paulo Wanchope really does trump Thierry Henry, when the Costa Rican's 50 top-flight goals take you up to the magic total of 442, but Henry puts you way out on 567. In the 442GOALS universe, all men are mostly equal – except for Alan Shearer, who will make or break your XI.

I scoured FourFourTwo's list of the greatest Premier League players ever and put all 100 into a database. You roll the dice and get three random players – enough to employ your ball knowledge, not too many to feel easy. Game done, basically – then I added another 100 players, and another 100.

I can promise you now, if you've played the game and found it even slightly addictive, imagine what it was like with the competition's history at your fingertips, and the urge to add new game modes, wildcards, Liam Rosenior and Arsenal's classic bruised banana kit.

It started well: the kind of viewing figures we get when FourFourTwo post a new Friday Football Quiz. Then I made a cup of tea, came back and it had doubled. Brilliant! I'll tweet about it and see if the people who follow me like it.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

Then things got out of hand: 250,000 views in 24 hours, and then a million 24 hours after that. I've only ever gone viral a couple of times before – once was for suggesting that Kevin Volland's name was actually Kevin Vetherlands – so I really wasn't used to, or expecting, any of it.

My inbox flooded with strangers' scores. The simple words “Cheers, you've ruined my day”, popping up on WhatsApp every hour. I felt like an ethical Mark Zuckerberg, with all of footballing time and space my Ivy League.

The response has been overwhelming. It's just a game – but the best thing about it is the life it's taken on since. People are getting in touch to tell me their lowest scores (someone managed just 17 goals, picking as many flops as possible), while one Everton fan online is trying to build a team solely of Toffees.

There are people who have hit more than 1,000 goals, people who have found the one outfield player secretly eligible to play in goal, and people trying to build a team that could realistically win a title.

So while I can share my advice with you on how to tame 442GOALS – build from the back, and save the Scout's IQ wildcard for your final roll – it's not really my game any more. It belongs to the bored, to those on their lunch breaks or commutes. All that's left to do is thank you for playing.

We're working on the next project, for the next time there's no football on…

He has written cover features for the mag on Mikel Arteta and Martin Odegaard, and is assisted by his cat, Rosie, who has interned for the brand since lockdown.
